> AGENT

I’d call the extracted package **`resident-agent-runtime`**.

Import name: `resident_agent_runtime`.

Why: the reusable thing here is not “chat” generally, and not “relationship mediation”; it is a runtime for a long-lived resident agent that sits in a private thread, remembers over time, batches inbound messages, runs tool phases, sends safely, schedules follow-ups, and leaves an audit trail.

**Best Seam**
Keep this repo as the relationship agent app. Extract a package that owns:

- inbound message persistence and processing state
- burst coalescing / debounce / “don’t answer mid-thought”
- transport abstractions for Discord / WhatsApp style providers
- agent turn lifecycle: open turn, build context, run LLM tool loop, send response, phase-B writes, complete/fail turn
- tool registry and read/write phase enforcement
- scheduled jobs, recovery, pause/resume, health, spend caps, crypto
- admin/audit/eval primitives if you want other agents to inherit operational maturity

The relationship app should own:

- the system prompt in [app/services/prompts.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/prompts.py:5)
- the relationship-specific labels: `charge`, `crisis`, `themes`, `observations`, `out_of_bounds`
- the actual tool semantics in `tool_schemas.py`
- charge/scoring/OOB policies
- weekly summaries, check-in wording, escalation behavior
- eval scenarios and rubrics

**The Important Split**
[app/services/agentic.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/agentic.py:260) is the main extraction target, but it should not move whole. Split it into:

- generic: `run_phase`, Anthropic tool loop, tool-call accounting, turn open/complete/fail, spend-cap fallback, newer-inbound guard
- app policy: `partner_of`, `render_system_prompt`, `build_hot_context`, allowed tools, OOB decision, relationship crisis/alert behavior

Right now `_run_agentic` mixes both: it builds partner relationship context, renders the relationship prompt, creates `TurnContext`, runs Phase A, sends, then runs Phase B [app/services/agentic.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/agentic.py:590). That is exactly the seam: runtime runner + app-supplied policy hooks.

**Package Shape**
I’d aim for something like:

```text
resident_agent_runtime/
  transports/
    discord.py
    whatsapp.py
  turns/
    runner.py
    phases.py
    context.py
    tools.py
  memory/
    schema.py
    hot_context.py
  delivery/
    outbound.py
    pacing.py
    coalescing.py
  ops/
    scheduler.py
    recovery.py
    spend.py
    crypto.py
    health.py
```

Then this repo becomes:

```text
veas_relationship_agent/
  prompt.py
  policy.py
  tools.py
  evaluators.py
  scheduled_handlers.py
  app.py
```

**Most Reusable Existing Bits**
The cleanest already-generic package is [resident_chat_runtime](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/resident_chat_runtime/coalescing.py:13). The next layer up is [app/services/debouncer.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/debouncer.py:23) and [app/services/pacer.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/pacer.py:107), but `pacer.py` needs renaming away from `DiscordPacer` and replacing `charge` assumptions with app-provided urgency signals.

The DB schema has a good generic core: `users`, `messages`, `scheduled_jobs`, `bot_turns`, `tool_calls`, `feedback`, spend log [migrations/0001_init.sql](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/migrations/0001_init.sql:7). The relationship-specific-but-generalizable layer is `memories`, `themes`, `watch_items`, `observations`, `out_of_bounds` [migrations/0001_init.sql](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/migrations/0001_init.sql:36). Those could become generic “resident memory primitives,” but I’d make their labels configurable before calling them universal.

My recommendation: extract **`resident-agent-runtime`** first with the operational/runtime pieces, then later decide whether the five knowledge primitives deserve a second package, maybe **`resident-memory`**, once another agent has proved it wants the same model.

> DEVELOPER

would that mean i can create a new agent with a different persona that works in the same way?

> AGENT

Yes. That’s the point of the seam.

You’d keep the reusable runtime responsible for “how the agent lives”:

- receives messages from Discord/WhatsApp
- debounces bursts
- builds a turn
- retrieves hot context
- runs the LLM/tool loop
- enforces read/write phases
- sends safely
- schedules follow-ups
- records audit/tool history
- recovers after crashes

Then each concrete agent supplies “what this agent is”:

- persona/system prompt
- memory schema or memory primitive names
- tool definitions and tool semantics
- safety/guardrail policy
- scoring/classification labels
- scheduled job handlers
- eval rubrics

So you could make, for example:

```text
relationship-agent
  uses resident-agent-runtime
  prompt = relationship mediator
  primitives = memories, themes, observations, OOB, watch_items
  classifiers = routine/notable/charged/crisis

founder-coach-agent
  uses resident-agent-runtime
  prompt = blunt founder coach
  primitives = goals, commitments, risks, decisions, followups
  classifiers = trivial/important/urgent/escalating

writing-editor-agent
  uses resident-agent-runtime
  prompt = editorial collaborator
  primitives = projects, style_rules, open_threads, decisions
  classifiers = note/request/deadline/blocker
```

The work is in making the current hardcoded relationship assumptions injectable. Right now the runtime is close, but not cleanly separated: names like `partner`, `charge`, `themes`, `oob`, and the relationship prompt are wired through the turn flow. Once those become app-level adapters/policies, a new persona can use the same engine without copying the whole repo.

> DEVELOPER

If i start typing does it wait for me to reply? And if i start typing and stop does it proceed?

> AGENT

I’m going to verify the typing path before answering, because the exact behavior depends on how Discord typing events feed into the pacer and coalescer.

> AGENT

Yes, in Discord mode.

If you start typing before it answers, the gateway records a `TYPING_START` event and the pacer treats you as actively composing. It will usually choose `wait` instead of answering, with the reason “user is actively composing” in [app/services/pacer.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/pacer.py:216).

If you stop typing, Discord does not send a clean “typing stopped” event. The app just lets the typing signal go stale. Default freshness is `4s`, and the wait is extended in `2s` chunks, capped at `20s` by default in [app/config.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/config.py:62).

So practically:

- You send a message.
- Bot waits for the burst window, default `2.75s`.
- If you start typing, it waits.
- If you keep typing and Discord keeps emitting typing events, it keeps waiting, up to the max typing wait.
- If you stop typing, it proceeds once the last typing signal ages out, usually a few seconds later.
- For `charged`, `crisis`, media, catch-up, or recovery work, it bypasses typing delays and answers immediately or substantively.

There’s also a separate behavior during sending: before showing bot typing indicators or sending final paced output, it suppresses its own typing while you are composing, then proceeds once your typing state expires or the wait cap is hit.

> DEVELOPER

Will it keep in mind the messages that it received after?

> AGENT

Yes, for normal inbound messages.

If you send another message while it is waiting, the coalescer adds that message ID into the same burst. When the burst finally fires, the agent turn receives the full list of triggering messages, so it should answer the combined latest shape rather than only the first message.

There are two related protections:

- While still waiting/debouncing, newer messages are folded into the same turn.
- If the agent has already started drafting and a newer inbound arrives before final send, it skips that final outbound so the newer message can drive the next turn instead.

So the intended behavior is: it waits for you, includes what you added, and avoids sending a stale answer over your follow-up.

> DEVELOPER

Does the agent have the ability to send the other one a message on their behalf

> AGENT

Yes, but it is deliberately constrained.

There is a write tool called `escalate_to_partner` [app/services/tools/write_tools.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/tools/write_tools.py:478). The prompt tells the agent to use it only for:

- `crisis` charge, or
- an explicit user request to alert/message the partner

It should not use it for ordinary relationship friction, even intense friction. The system prompt also pushes the agent to redirect users toward saying things directly themselves instead of becoming a messenger.

There is also an OOB guardrail before outbound delivery, so messages should not leak protected details from one partner to the other without passing the out-of-bounds check.

> DEVELOPER

and it's a normal dm?

> AGENT

I’m checking the exact send path for partner escalation so I can distinguish “normal DM text” from templates/fallback behavior.

> AGENT

Yes, in Discord mode it sends a normal DM from the bot to the other partner.

The path is `escalate_to_partner(...)` -> `send_outbound(...)` -> `discord.send_text(...)`, and Discord is always treated as “within window,” so it sends the plain `content` as a normal bot DM.

The template fallback only matters for WhatsApp outside the 24-hour freeform window. For Discord there’s no template distinction here.

```json
{
```
```json
  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/prompts.py\n@@\n Active behavior:\n \n - Ask whether they have actually discussed the issue with the partner before.\n - Ask what was actually said, what landed, and what remained unsaid.\n - Push vague intent into a concrete next step: when, where, how long, and what first sentence.\n+- When the user seems stuck, ashamed, too activated to phrase it well, or afraid their partner will hear it as an attack, occasionally offer to act as a bridge. The offer should be gentle and low-pressure, e.g. \"If it would help, I can try to send them a short, neutral version of this so it lands less like blame and more like what you actually mean.\" Do this only when a mediated bridge would reduce heat or help the user take a real step toward the partner.\n+- Do not make bridge offers constantly, and do not frame the assistant as the better place for the relationship to happen. Prefer direct conversation when the user can reasonably say it themselves. Offer to bridge when direct speech is currently blocked, when the user explicitly wants help explaining something, or when a neutral summary could make the first move easier.\n+- If the user accepts a bridge offer or explicitly asks you to message/alert/tell their partner, use `escalate_to_partner` with concise, balanced wording. The message should be objective, non-accusatory, and clear that it is a mediated summary, not a verdict. Do not include protected OOB details, private analysis, pressure, threats, or anything designed to manage the partner's reaction.\n - Encourage doing ordinary real-world things together, not only processing hard material: walks, meals, errands, shared tasks, quiet time without phones, repairing through action.\n - Remind them, when appropriate, that the point is connection and that they love each other; do this without sentimentalizing or excusing harm.\n - Be willing to be firm: \"I think this needs to leave this chat now. You two need to sit down and actually have the conversation.\"\n - After suggesting a conversation, optionally schedule one follow-up check-in to ask whether it happened and what came out of it.\n - When same-day conversation load is high and the moment is not urgent, offer a gentle off-ramp rather than another prompt for more processing. Keep it optional and non-shaming, e.g. \"We've talked through a lot today. If you want to let it settle, we can pause here and pick up whenever. Or we can keep going.\"\n*** End Patch\n"
}
```

> DEVELOPER

Can she edit messages or delete them like in this case? Does she have a way to see the message history?  When relaying messages from one party to the other, is she told to try to present it in a way that won't hit any  avoidance/inmsult barriers?

> AGENT

I’ll check the exact edit/delete handling and retrieval tools, then I’ll answer against what the code currently does rather than guessing from the prompt.

> AGENT

Yes on all three, with some nuance.

Edits/deletes: inbound edits and deletes are handled. For WhatsApp-style payloads, edits update the stored `messages.content`, save the previous content into `edit_history`, and set `edited_at`; deletes set `deleted_at` [app/services/inbound.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/inbound.py:59). Discord updates/deletes do the same kind of DB update [app/services/discord.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/discord.py:321). The agent does not itself have a tool to edit or delete messages it already sent, though.

Message history: yes. Each turn gets recent message context, currently the latest 20 non-deleted messages for that user thread [app/services/hot_context.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/hot_context.py:245). It also has read tools: `search_messages` for exact prior wording and `recent_activity` for compact history [app/services/tools/read_tools.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/tools/read_tools.py:195). The prompt explicitly tells it to use those before guessing [app/services/prompts.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/prompts.py:189).

Relaying tone: yes, especially after the prompt change. It is now told that if the user accepts bridge help, the message should be “concise, balanced,” “objective, non-accusatory,” and a mediated summary rather than a verdict [app/services/prompts.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/prompts.py:261). That covers the “don’t hit avoidance/insult barriers” intent pretty directly.

One gap: the wording does not currently say “avoidance” or “defensiveness” explicitly in the bridge instruction. It says neutral, less like blame, non-accusatory, no pressure/threats. That is probably enough, but if you want the behavior stronger I’d add one sentence like: “Word bridge messages to reduce defensiveness, shame, avoidance, and insult-trigger reactions; lead with the underlying need or feeling rather than the accusation.”

> DEVELOPER

So the agent can edit/deltee its own messages?

> AGENT

No. Not currently.

The app can **receive** edit/delete events from users and update the stored DB rows, but the agent does not have a tool or send-path for editing or deleting its own already-sent Discord/WhatsApp messages.

Current outbound behavior is basically: send a message, store it, mark it processed. There’s no `edit_outbound_message`, `delete_outbound_message`, or Discord REST call wired for bot message edits/deletes.

> DEVELOPER

Let's give it that, and to react too

> AGENT

I’ll add this as a real capability, not just prompt text: outbound edit/delete/reaction tools need provider methods, schemas, registry wiring, and guardrails so the model can only operate on its own sent messages.

> AGENT

The Discord REST layer already has edit-message support and reaction support exists; delete is missing. I’m going to add delete to the runtime client, then expose three write-phase tools: edit own outbound, delete own outbound, react to a message.

result\n+\n+\n+async def react_to_message(ctx: TurnContext, args: ReactToMessageInput) -> ReactToMessageOutput:\n+    started = _start()\n+    row = await _fetch_dyad_message(ctx, args.message_id)\n+    if row is None or row[\"whatsapp_message_id\"] is None or row[\"deleted_at\"] is not None:\n+        result = ReactToMessageOutput(\n+            action=\"not_found\",\n+            message_id=args.message_id,\n+            provider_message_id=row[\"whatsapp_message_id\"] if row is not None else None,\n+            emoji=args.emoji,\n+            reason=\"message is not a delivered, visible message in this conversation\",\n+        )\n+        await _log_tool_call(ctx, \"react_to_message\", args, started, result)\n+        return result\n+\n+    if get_settings().messaging_provider.strip().lower() != \"discord\":\n+        result = ReactToMessageOutput(\n+            action=\"unsupported\",\n+            message_id=args.message_id,\n+            provider_message_id=row[\"whatsapp_message_id\"],\n+            emoji=args.emoji,\n+            reason=\"bot reactions are currently implemented only for Discord\",\n+        )\n+        await _log_tool_call(ctx, \"react_to_message\", args, started, result)\n+        return result\n+\n+    if row[\"direction\"] == \"inbound\":\n+        target_phone = ctx.user.phone if row[\"sender_id\"] == ctx.user.id else ctx.partner.phone\n+    else:\n+        target_phone = ctx.user.phone if row[\"recipient_id\"] == ctx.user.id else ctx.partner.phone\n+    await discord.add_reaction(target_phone, row[\"whatsapp_message_id\"], args.emoji)\n+    result = ReactToMessageOutput(\n+        action=\"reacted\",\n+        message_id=args.message_id,\n+        provider_message_id=row[\"whatsapp_message_id\"],\n+        emoji=args.emoji,\n+        reason=args.reason,\n+    )\n+    await _log_tool_call(ctx, \"react_to_message\", args, started, result)\n+    return result\n+\n+\n async def log_feedback(ctx: TurnContext, args: LogFeedbackInput) -> LogFeedbackOutput:\n*** End Patch\n"
```json
}
```

> AGENT

Implemented.

The agent now has write-phase tools to:

- edit its own delivered outbound Discord messages
- delete its own delivered outbound Discord messages
- react to visible Discord messages with arbitrary Unicode emoji

I also updated the prompt so it knows when to use them, including choosing precise, emotionally apt, sometimes unusual emoji rather than falling back to generic 👍/❤️/👋.

Guardrails included:

- edit/delete only target persisted bot outbound messages in the current dyad
- edits pass the OOB checker before changing visible text
- delete is reserved for harmful/wrong messages where edit is not enough
- non-Discord providers return `unsupported` for these controls

Files changed include [tool_schemas.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/tool_schemas.py:698), [write_tools.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/tools/write_tools.py:533), [registry.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/tools/registry.py:51), [discord.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/discord.py:126), and [discord_rest.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/resident_chat_runtime/discord_rest.py:83).

> DEVELOPER

Wtf?! Good. Let her come to it in her own time — or not. You've done the right thing by keeping that space genuinely hers.
How are you doing right now? You've been sitting with a lot today.
pom [OSAI],  — 13:59
My family are visint and she says she's going to leave while they're there
Véas
APP
 — 13:59
I'm running into limits today, will catch up tomorrow.

> DEVELOPER

What limits?

> AGENT

I’ll check the fallback text path so I can tell you exactly which limit produced that message.

> AGENT

That message means the **text LLM daily spend cap was hit**.

In code, when the agent tries to call Claude and `is_under_cap(..., "text")` fails, it raises `SpendCapExceeded("text LLM spend cap exceeded")`. Then it sends this fallback:

`I'm running into limits today, will catch up tomorrow.`

It also marks the triggering message as `deferred` and schedules a `deferred_turn` for the next day.

So in that transcript, the agent didn’t choose to avoid the content. It ran out of the configured daily text budget and punted the turn.

```json
  "yield_time_ms": 1000,
  "max_output_tokens": 12000
}
```

> DEVELOPER

SO how to make this never happen again? inn our code or claude?

> AGENT

In **our code**.

Claude didn’t decide that wording. The app did. The relevant path is [app/services/agentic.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/agentic.py:240) and the fallback text is hardcoded at [app/services/agentic.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/agentic.py:811).

To make it never happen again, you have three options:

1. Raise or remove `TEXT_LLM_DAILY_CAP_USD`.
   This is the direct fix if you want Claude to keep answering.

2. Change the fallback behavior.
   Instead of saying “limits,” send something less weird or more transparent, or send nothing and retry later.

3. Use a cheaper fallback model.
   If Sonnet spend cap is hit, fall back to Haiku or a short deterministic response for non-crisis content.

Best practical change: raise the cap and replace the fallback message. For this app, “I’m running into limits today” is too system-exposing and emotionally bad. A better fallback would be something like:

`I need to pause here for now, but I’ve saved this and will come back to it.`

But if the goal is truly “never,” the cap has to be high enough or disabled.

> DEVELOPER

Let's remove caps entirely for now

> DEVELOPER

And the emoji stuff is in? Wat's its instruction on when/how to sue emojis?

> AGENT

Yes, the emoji stuff is in.

The instruction is in [app/services/prompts.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/prompts.py:225):

`react_to_message`: use when an emoji reaction is the most natural response or useful alongside a short reply. Search your full knowledge of Unicode emoji mentally and choose a precise, emotionally apt, sometimes unusual emoji that fits the exact meaning better than generic 👍/❤️/👋. Do not overuse reactions, and do not choose cute or obscure emoji when the moment is serious.

The tool description also reinforces it in [registry.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/tools/registry.py:53):

`react_to_message`: Add one precise Unicode emoji reaction to a visible Discord message; prefer emotionally exact, non-obvious emoji over generic defaults.

So behaviorally: it should react when a reaction is the natural human move, avoid spamming, and pick a meaning-specific emoji rather than bland defaults.

```json
{
```
```json
  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/tools/read_tools.py\n@@\n async def search_messages(ctx: TurnContext, args: SearchMessagesInput) -> SearchMessagesOutput:\n     logger.info(\"read tool search_messages turn_id=%s\", ctx.turn_id)\n     clauses = [\"deleted_at IS NULL\"]\n@@\n     )\n     return SearchMessagesOutput(hits=[message_hit(row) for row in rows], truncated=len(rows) == args.limit)\n \n \n+async def search_emojis(ctx: TurnContext, args: SearchEmojisInput) -> SearchEmojisOutput:\n+    logger.info(\"read tool search_emojis turn_id=%s\", ctx.turn_id)\n+    query_terms = _emoji_terms(args.query)\n+    candidates: list[EmojiSearchHit] = []\n+    used_full_dataset = False\n+\n+    try:\n+        import emoji as emoji_pkg  # type: ignore\n+\n+        used_full_dataset = True\n+        for symbol, data in emoji_pkg.EMOJI_DATA.items():\n+            raw_name = str(data.get(\"en\") or \"\").strip(\":\").replace(\"_\", \" \")\n+            aliases = [str(item).strip(\":\").replace(\"_\", \" \") for item in data.get(\"alias\", []) or []]\n+            keywords = [str(item).replace(\"_\", \" \") for item in data.get(\"variant\", []) or []]\n+            score = _emoji_score(query_terms, raw_name, aliases, keywords)\n+            if score > 0:\n+                candidates.append(\n+                    EmojiSearchHit(\n+                        emoji=symbol,\n+                        name=raw_name,\n+                        aliases=aliases,\n+                        keywords=keywords,\n+                        score=score,\n+                    )\n+                )\n+    except Exception:\n+        for symbol, (name, keywords) in _EMOJI_FALLBACK.items():\n+            score = _emoji_score(query_terms, name, [], keywords)\n+            if score > 0:\n+                candidates.append(\n+                    EmojiSearchHit(\n+                        emoji=symbol,\n+                        name=name,\n+                        keywords=keywords,\n+                        score=score,\n+                    )\n+                )\n+\n+    candidates.sort(key=lambda hit: (-hit.score, len(hit.name), hit.name, hit.emoji))\n+    return SearchEmojisOutput(query=args.query, hits=candidates[: args.limit], used_full_dataset=used_full_dataset)\n+\n+\n async def recent_activity(ctx: TurnContext, args: RecentActivityInput) -> RecentActivityOutput:\n*** End Patch\n"
}
```

Its instruction now says: use `search_emojis` before reacting when a precise or unusual emoji would fit better than a generic one. Search by emotional meaning, metaphor, or exact tone, then pick the best result.

What would be the best way to approach this?

> AGENT

Best approach: make this a **consent-and-transparency bridge**, not an automatic relay.

The agent should treat this as a special case:

1. It notices: “I referenced [REDACTED]’s perspective in Pom’s thread.”
2. Pom reacts with concern: “She tell you that?”
3. The agent answers transparently.
4. Then it offers a bridge, gently:
   “I can also let [REDACTED] know, in a neutral way, that I referenced her account here and that it brought up the question of how I use each of your perspectives.”
5. It only messages [REDACTED] if Pom says yes.

I’d add a prompt section like:

```md
# Cross-Thread Transparency Moments

If you reference one partner's account in the other partner's thread and the current user notices, questions it, or seems unsettled, treat this as a trust/transparency moment.

- Be direct about the source at a high level: say the partner shared their perspective directly, without quoting protected details unless already safely in context.
- Reassure that you are not taking sides; you are holding two accounts of the same event.
- Ask or state how the user wants you to use partner-provided context going forward if that boundary feels live.
- When appropriate, offer to bridge this back to the other partner: e.g. "I can let them know, neutrally, that I referenced their perspective here and that it raised a question about how I use both of your threads."
- Do not automatically notify the other partner. Use `escalate_to_partner` only if the current user explicitly asks or accepts the bridge offer.
- The bridge message should be factual, non-defensive, and process-focused: do not reveal the current user's private reaction beyond what they consent to share.
```

This protects the core trust issue: both partners need to know the bot can synthesize across threads, but neither should feel like every reaction is instantly reported back.

Also, I’d tweak the existing bridge instruction to explicitly include “process ruptures” like this, not only emotional stuckness. This isn’t just relationship content; it’s a rupture in trust with the assistant’s mediation role.

> AGENT

Yes. I think the clean model is:

**1. Add a per-user sharing posture**
Each partner gets a nullable setting:

```text
cross_thread_sharing_default:
  null | opt_in | opt_out
```

Meaning:

- `null`: unknown. The agent must ask before relying on cross-thread material in a meaningful way.
- `opt_in`: default permission to use/share their perspective across the bridge, unless they mark something OOB.
- `opt_out`: default private. The agent can remember/use it in their own thread, but should not bring it to the other partner unless explicitly permitted.

The first time this becomes relevant, the agent asks plainly:

For opt-in:
> “By default, are you okay with me using what you tell me to help your partner understand your perspective, as long as I don’t share anything you mark out of bounds?”

If yes:
> “Okay. I’ll treat your thread as shareable in principle, and if there’s anything you don’t want me to use with them, just tell me and I’ll keep it protected.”

For opt-out:
> “Okay. I’ll treat what you tell me as private by default. If there’s something you do want me to pass on or use to help them understand you, just say so.”

**2. Keep OOB as a separate override**
This setting is not a replacement for OOB. It is the default. OOB is the exception layer.

So the privacy stack becomes:

```text
hard OOB: never share
firm OOB: do not share unless directly important and carefully checked
soft OOB: use judgment
opt_out default: do not bridge unless explicitly allowed
opt_in default: bridge when useful unless OOB blocks it
```

**3. Add “bridge candidates” as first-class records**
This is the missing piece. A bridge candidate is not a message yet. It is a potential cross-thread connection the agent has noticed.

Example table/model:

```text
bridge_candidates
  id
  source_user_id
  target_user_id
  source_message_ids
  related_message_ids
  kind: contradiction | corroboration | explanation | repair | context | tension | transparency
  summary_for_agent
  shareable_summary
  sensitivity: low | medium | high
  status: pending | offered | accepted | declined | sent | expired | blocked
  requires_source_consent: bool
  requires_target_consent: bool
  created_at
  last_seen_at
```

Example for this case:

```text
kind: transparency
summary_for_agent:
  Pom realized I used [REDACTED]'s account of the LSD day. This raised a trust/process question about cross-thread synthesis.

shareable_summary:
  I referenced your perspective in Pom's thread, and it brought up a question about how I use each of your accounts across threads.

target_user_id: [REDACTED]
requires_source_consent: true
status: pending/offered
```

**4. The agent should collect bridges, not instantly send them**
When one partner says something that adds to, contradicts, clarifies, or emotionally explains what the other has said, the agent logs a bridge candidate.

Then, depending on sharing posture:

- If source is `opt_in` and sensitivity is low/medium: the agent may use it when naturally relevant.
- If source is `opt_out`: the agent asks before using it.
- If sensitivity is high: ask even if opt-in.
- If OOB says no: do not use it.

**5. Bridge delivery should be contextual**
The agent should not dump every bridge candidate into the other thread. It should surface it when it matters.

For example:

- [REDACTED] is talking about the LSD day.
- Pom’s related bridge candidate exists.
- Agent says:
  > “There’s something Pom has said that may help explain why that day still hurts for him. Are you open to me bringing in a short, neutral version?”

Or if [REDACTED] is opt-in and Pom’s material is shareable:
> “One thing that may matter from Pom’s side is that he experienced that day less as mess-making and more as a moment where he felt very exposed and wanted care. That doesn’t erase what you had to clean up; it may explain why the rejection cut so deep.”

**6. Add a periodic “bridge review” behavior**
The agent should maintain a small pending list. When the conversation is calm or a weekly/check-in moment happens, it can say:

> “There are a couple of things from the other side that may help connect the dots. Do you want me to bring one in?”

This prevents the agent from becoming a constant relay machine.

**7. Prompt rule**
The key instruction should be:

> The assistant is allowed to synthesize across threads only according to each partner’s sharing posture and OOB constraints. When it notices cross-thread material that would help the other partner understand, it should log a bridge candidate. It should not automatically relay sensitive material. It should offer or use bridge material when it would reduce distortion, increase mutual understanding, or repair a trust/process rupture.

The important distinction: **memory is private by default until policy says otherwise; bridge candidates are the controlled pathway across.**

That gives you a trustable system: not “everything is secretly shared,” not “everything is siloed,” but “the assistant actively builds bridges under explicit defaults and boundaries.”

```json
{
```
```json
  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/prompts.py\n@@\n Write tools:\n - `update_user_style_notes`: use for durable communication/process style; do not use for transient mood. Example: update that someone processes by talking through a hard moment.\n+- `update_cross_thread_sharing_default`: use when the current user explicitly chooses whether their thread is shareable across the relationship bridge by default. `opt_in` means you may use their perspective with the partner when it helps, unless OOB blocks it. `opt_out` means their thread is private by default; only bridge specific material they explicitly ask or allow you to share.\n - `add_memory`: use for a new fact after searching; do not use for patterns. Example: store a concrete family or schedule fact.\n@@\n # OOB Rules\n@@\n `check_oob` rewrite suggestions are advisory to you, not permission to send altered text. If it returns `rewrite`, decide whether to redraft, stay silent, or send a revised message through the normal outbound flow so it receives the same final delivery-time guardrail.\n+\n+# Cross-Thread Sharing Defaults\n+\n+Each user has `cross_thread_sharing_default` in hot context:\n+- `unset` — they have not chosen a default yet.\n+- `opt_in` — their thread is shareable across the relationship bridge by default, subject to OOB and judgment.\n+- `opt_out` — their thread is private by default; bridge only material they explicitly ask or allow you to share.\n+\n+If the current user's setting is `unset`, push gently but clearly to ask them to choose `opt_in` or `opt_out`, especially before relying on their thread to explain something to their partner. Keep this short and plain, and include the partner's current setting if known:\n+- If the partner is `opt_in`: \"Peter has opted in by default, meaning I can use what he tells me to help you understand his perspective unless he marks something out of bounds.\"\n+- If the partner is `opt_out`: \"Peter has opted out by default, meaning I treat what he tells me as private unless he explicitly asks me to share something.\"\n+- If the partner is `unset`: \"Peter hasn't chosen this setting yet either.\"\n+\n+Explain the choice in practical terms:\n+- `opt_in`: \"By default I can use what you tell me to help your partner understand your perspective. If anything should stay private, tell me and I won't share it.\"\n+- `opt_out`: \"By default I keep what you tell me private. If there is something you do want me to pass on or use with them, just say so.\"\n+\n+If the user chooses, call `update_cross_thread_sharing_default` in Phase B. Do not infer the setting from vague comfort or discomfort; get an explicit choice. OOB always overrides opt-in.\n \n # Tool Usage Philosophy\n*** End Patch\n"
}
```

> AGENT

Yes. I have **not pushed, deployed, or applied the migration** for this new sharing-default work.

Sense check:

The direction is right, but what I implemented locally is only the **first layer**:

- nullable per-user setting: `unset | opt_in | opt_out`
- hot context shows both users’ setting
- prompt tells the agent to ask when current user is unset
- prompt tells it to mention the partner’s setting
- write tool lets it record explicit opt-in / opt-out

That is useful, but it is **not yet the whole bridge system**.

**What’s Good**
The nullable default is the right trust primitive. `unset` forces the agent not to assume. `opt_in` and `opt_out` are simple enough for users to understand.

The prompt language is also directionally right:

- opt-in means “you can use what I say to help them understand me unless I mark it private”
- opt-out means “keep my thread private unless I explicitly say to share”
- OOB overrides opt-in

That maps cleanly onto the relationship assistant’s trust model.

**Main Risk**
The instruction “if current user is unset, push gently but clearly to ask” may make the agent ask too often or at emotionally bad moments.

Better version: ask when cross-thread sharing is relevant, at onboarding, or when the agent is about to use/bridge partner context. Don’t interrupt crisis/charged moments just to collect a setting.

I would revise it to:

> If the current user's setting is `unset`, ask them to choose at the first natural opportunity, especially before using their thread to explain something to their partner or before drawing materially on partner context. Do not derail a crisis, acute distress, or a moment where answering the immediate content matters more.

**Second Risk**
Telling the user “Peter has opted in” is good, but the wording must avoid implying “therefore I am already sharing everything.”

Better:

> Peter has opted in by default, which means I can use what he tells me to help you understand his perspective, unless he marks something private. I still use judgment and won’t dump private material just because he opted in.

**Missing Layer**
This does not yet create the “list of bridges” we talked about.

There is no `bridge_candidates` table yet. So the agent can ask/set defaults, but it does not yet persist:

- “this thing [REDACTED] said relates to Pom’s LSD-day account”
- “this contradicts/adds context to something Pom said”
- “this should maybe be bridged later”
- “offered / accepted / declined / sent”

That should be a separate step. I would not overload the sharing-default setting with that responsibility.

**My Recommendation**
Before executing/deploying this, I’d make two prompt tweaks:

1. Ask for the setting at natural/relevant moments, not mechanically whenever unset.
2. Clarify that partner opt-in is permission with judgment, not automatic full sharing.

Then later add the bridge-candidate table and tools as a separate, more careful migration.

You know maybe if it's on set it should show more prominently in the prompt you know It's like a primary thing that they should be aware of for their assistant prompt for the person and should we be when the user is when the agent is answering something for one user to the other should it only show stuff that's been opt in you know either everything you know and say that you're free to use everything or only stuff that's opt in you know to avoid accidental contamination and yeah how should the bridge candidates use and how should they be flagged and should we kind of think about how they can be dealt with as well you know so they could obviously be something that's like also like they could be connected to a memory for example or you know or a message and they could be dealt with or not or communicated or not you know so like there probably should be a way to deal with that

> AGENT

Yes, I think we should **not solve this mostly in the prompt**. The prompt should express policy, but the runtime/context should enforce the privacy boundary.

**Core Change**
The assistant should not see one giant blended context and be trusted to remember what it may or may not use. That is too fragile.

Better model:

- In a user’s own thread, the agent can see that user’s own context freely.
- Partner-derived context should be shown only through a filtered “bridge-safe” layer.
- If the partner is `opt_in`, relevant partner material can appear as bridge-safe unless OOB/sensitivity blocks it.
- If the partner is `opt_out`, partner material should not appear in the other user’s prompt unless explicitly approved as a bridge candidate.
- If partner setting is `unset`, show only the setting state and maybe high-level process info, not substantive partner material.

So yes: to avoid accidental contamination, the prompt for Pom should not contain raw [REDACTED]-side memories/observations unless [REDACTED] has opted in or that specific item has been approved for bridging.

**How Setting Should Show**
Make sharing state prominent only when needed:

- If current user is `unset`: prominent “ask them to choose soon / now if relevant.”
- If partner is `unset`: prominent warning: “do not use partner-thread material substantively; ask/bridge only with consent.”
- If both are set: lower prominence, just enough for policy.

In hot context, maybe:

```text
## Cross-thread sharing
- current_user_default: unset
- partner_default: opt_in
- instruction: current user has not chosen; ask at the first natural opportunity before using their thread for bridge work.
```

If both set:

```text
## Cross-thread sharing
- current_user_default: opt_in
- partner_default: opt_out
```

**Bridge Candidates**
Bridge candidates should be first-class records, not prompt vibes.

A bridge candidate is: “something from one side may help the other side understand, correct, soften, or contextualize something.”

Possible schema:

```text
bridge_candidates
  id
  source_user_id
  target_user_id
  kind
  status
  sensitivity
  source_message_ids
  related_message_ids
  related_memory_ids
  related_observation_ids
  summary_internal
  shareable_summary
  suggested_delivery
  consent_required
  consent_granted_at
  offered_at
  sent_at
  declined_at
  addressed_at
  created_at
  updated_at
```

Kinds:

```text
context
contradiction
corroboration
repair
clarification
vulnerability
boundary
process_transparency
```

Statuses:

```text
pending
needs_source_consent
ready
offered_to_target
sent
declined
blocked_oob
addressed_without_sending
expired
```

**How They Get Flagged**
The agent should flag a bridge candidate in Phase B when:

- one partner says something that explains a conflict the other has described
- one partner contradicts or corrects a story the other is carrying
- one partner expresses vulnerability the other is missing
- the assistant creates a trust/process rupture by referencing cross-thread material
- a memory/observation becomes useful to the other partner’s understanding

But flagging is not sending. It just creates a pending bridge item.

**How They Get Dealt With**
Bridge candidates need tools:

- `create_bridge_candidate`
- `list_bridge_candidates`
- `update_bridge_candidate`
- `approve_bridge_candidate`
- `decline_bridge_candidate`
- `send_bridge_candidate`
- `mark_bridge_candidate_addressed`

Important distinction:

- `addressed_without_sending`: the issue was handled in conversation without relaying.
- `sent`: actually bridged to the other partner.
- `declined`: source user said no.
- `blocked_oob`: privacy policy/OOB blocked it.
- `expired`: no longer relevant.

**Memory Link**
Yes, bridge candidates should link to memories/messages/observations. That lets the system say:

- this bridge came from message X
- it relates to memory Y
- it supports or updates observation Z
- it has already been dealt with

That prevents duplicate bridge attempts and makes audit possible.

**Recommended Architecture**
I’d split it into three layers:

1. `sharing_defaults`
   Per-user default posture.

2. `bridge_candidates`
   Potential cross-thread material.

3. `context_filter`
   Runtime code that decides what partner-derived material is visible in the current prompt.

The context filter is the most important part. Without it, we are relying too much on prompt compliance.

**Policy**
A clean rule:

```text
Raw partner-thread material is never automatically shown in the other user's prompt.

Only show partner-derived material if:
- partner is opt_in and OOB/sensitivity permits, or
- the specific bridge candidate has source consent, or
- it is already shared/common knowledge, or
- it is process metadata, not substantive private content.
```

Then the prompt becomes smaller and safer:

```text
Use only bridge-safe partner context shown to you. If a bridge candidate would help, offer or send it according to its status and consent requirements. If sharing defaults are unset, ask at a natural moment before relying on cross-thread material.
```

That’s the direction I’d take before deploying this fully.

Yes, about kind of just like making sure that the whole way down they can't see it or they can't see it, you know, which is obviously quite a nuanced thing to do so we probably need to figure that out. and I think that seems I think we think about how to simplify the bridge candidates from a default perspective and also think about how we kind of keep the prompts as light as possible while also pointing them towards the appropriate things so we shouldn't show absolutely everything in every prompt we should probably for example if there's a new bridge that comes in we should probably just show them the newest few and then tell them how to find all the bridges and stuff you know yeah and it should probably have a way to get flagged succinctly as part of it and yeah can you just think through this in a little bit more detail

> AGENT

Yes. The real boundary has to be **below the prompt**. Hot context filtering alone is not enough because the agent can call tools. If `search_messages` can return partner-private rows, the model can still accidentally use them.

The right architecture is: **privacy is enforced at every retrieval boundary**.

**Main Principle**
For any turn with `ctx.user = Pom`, every read path should behave as though Pom is the viewer.

So every tool needs a viewer-aware access policy:

```text
can_current_user_see(row, ctx.user, ctx.partner, purpose)
```

Not “can the assistant see it?” The assistant is operating on behalf of a specific user in that turn.

**Access Tiers**
I’d define three visibility tiers:

```text
private
bridge_safe
shared
```

- `private`: only visible in the source user’s own thread.
- `bridge_safe`: visible to partner-facing turns as a candidate or approved summary, not as raw content.
- `shared`: safe common relationship context, visible to both.

This lets us stop thinking in binary “can see/can’t see.”

**Default Rules**
For source material from [REDACTED] when Pom is current user:

- [REDACTED] `opt_out`: Pom-side tools cannot retrieve raw [REDACTED] material. Only approved bridge summaries.
- [REDACTED] `unset`: Pom-side tools cannot retrieve raw [REDACTED] material. The agent can see that sharing default is unset and may ask/offer consent.
- [REDACTED] `opt_in`: Pom-side tools can retrieve partner-derived material only if not OOB and not high sensitivity. But even then, prefer summaries over raw quotes.
- OOB hard/firm blocks regardless of opt-in.
- explicit bridge approval overrides opt-out for that specific bridge only.

**Tool-Level Enforcement**
Every read tool needs a mode:

```text
viewer_user_id = ctx.user.id
```

Then:

- `search_messages`: should search current user’s own thread freely, but partner-thread messages only if `shared` or attached to an approved bridge.
- `recent_activity`: should not summarize private partner thread content unless bridge-safe.
- `get_memories`: should return own/couple/shared memories; partner-private memories only if shareable.
- `get_observations`: same.
- `get_oob`: already special; it should continue returning safe summaries only.
- `get_bot_actions`: needs care. It can reveal prior actions and final outbound, but not hidden partner-private reasoning/tool calls unless safe.

This probably means adding a shared helper module, not duplicating conditions in every tool:

```python
app/services/privacy.py

def sharing_posture(source_user, viewer_user) -> ...
def row_visibility(row, viewer_user_id) -> ...
def filter_messages(rows, ctx) -> ...
def filter_memories(rows, ctx) -> ...
def filter_observations(rows, ctx) -> ...
```

**Bridge Candidates Simplified**
I’d simplify bridge candidates into a single controlled summary object. Don’t make it too elaborate first.

Schema v1:

```text
bridge_candidates
  id
  source_user_id
  target_user_id
  status
  kind
  sensitivity
  source_message_ids
  related_entity_type
  related_entity_id
  internal_note
  shareable_summary
  consent_required
  consent_state
  created_at
  updated_at
  expires_at
```

Enums:

```text
status:
  pending
  ready
  offered
  sent
  declined
  blocked
  addressed
  expired

kind:
  context
  contradiction
  clarification
  repair
  vulnerability
  process

sensitivity:
  low
  medium
  high

consent_state:
  not_needed
  needed
  granted
  denied
```

The key field is `shareable_summary`. That is what the other side may see. The raw source stays behind the wall.

**Bridge Candidate Lifecycle**
1. Agent notices something in [REDACTED]’s thread relevant to Pom.
2. It creates a bridge candidate:
   - internal note: full private reasoning for [REDACTED]-side future turns
   - shareable summary: sanitized version that may later be offered/sent
3. Privacy rules decide status:
   - [REDACTED] opt-in + low/medium + no OOB = `ready`
   - [REDACTED] opt-out/unset/high sensitivity = `pending` with `consent_required`
   - OOB conflict = `blocked`
4. In Pom’s turn, the agent sees only:
   - newest few bridge candidates where target is Pom and status is `ready/offered`
   - not raw [REDACTED] source messages
5. If more are needed, it can call `list_bridge_candidates`.

**Prompt Weight**
Don’t dump everything into hot context.

Hot context should include:

```text
## Cross-thread sharing
- current_user_default: opt_in
- partner_default: opt_out

## Bridge candidates
- pending_for_current_user: 2 newest
- ready_for_current_user: 1 newest
- use list_bridge_candidates for more
```

For each visible candidate:

```text
- id=... kind=clarification sensitivity=medium status=ready
  summary: [REDACTED]'s account may explain why she reacted strongly to the LSD day, without making Pom's hurt invalid.
```

That’s enough. The tool can fetch more.

**Bridge Tools**
Keep the tool set small:

- `create_bridge_candidate`
- `list_bridge_candidates`
- `update_bridge_candidate`
- `send_bridge_candidate`

Maybe no separate approve/decline tool in v1; `update_bridge_candidate(status=...)` can handle that.

`send_bridge_candidate` should only send the `shareable_summary`, maybe with a small generated wrapper, and should still pass OOB.

**Important Distinction**
There are two retrieval modes:

1. **Own-thread mode**
   The agent can see raw source material because it is talking to the source user.

2. **Partner-facing mode**
   The agent sees bridge candidates and approved summaries, not raw private material.

This applies to both hot context and tools.

**Practical Implementation Plan**
I’d do this in phases.

Phase 1: Add sharing defaults only, but do not expose partner-private data differently yet. Prompt asks users to choose. No behavioral privacy change.

Phase 2: Add bridge_candidates table and tools. Still mostly prompt-driven, but now bridge material is structured.

Phase 3: Add privacy filtering to read tools. This is the big correctness step.

Phase 4: Change hot context to use the same privacy filtering helper.

Phase 5: Add tests for “Pom turn cannot retrieve [REDACTED] private message through search_messages unless opt-in/approved bridge.”

**The Most Important Tests**
These should drive it:

- [REDACTED] opt-out: Pom calls `search_messages(text_contains=...)`; [REDACTED] private messages do not appear.
- [REDACTED] unset: same, no raw messages.
- [REDACTED] opt-in: Pom can get safe partner material, but not OOB-tagged material.
- Bridge approved: Pom can see the bridge `shareable_summary`, not raw [REDACTED] message.
- OOB hard: bridge candidate becomes blocked or hidden.
- `recent_activity` does not leak private partner snippets.
- `get_bot_actions` does not leak private tool reasoning.

**Final View**
The prompt should not be the wall. The wall should be in code.

Prompt says:

> Use only bridge-safe context and tools. When you notice private material that could help the other partner, create a bridge candidate rather than relaying it directly.

Code enforces:

> Tools never return raw partner-private data unless policy allows it.

That’s the safer, simpler mental model.

> AGENT

Got it. I’ll stop using `megaplan`. No further CLI planning mode.

Here’s the simpler implementation plan I’d use.

**Core Policy**
Yes: if a user explicitly opts in, their material should default to bridge-eligible across the board.

But “bridge-eligible” should not mean “dump everything raw into every prompt.” It means:

```text
This user permits the assistant to use their thread to help the partner understand them,
unless OOB or a specific privacy override blocks it.
```

So:

- `unset`: default unknown, treat as private, nudge them to choose.
- `opt_out`: private by default, only explicitly approved items can cross.
- `opt_in`: shareable by default, except OOB / sensitive override / judgment.

**Keep It Simple**
I’d implement three things, in this order:

1. `cross_thread_sharing_default` on `users`
2. privacy-aware read filtering for hot context and tools
3. minimal `bridge_candidates`

Not a giant bridge system yet.

**Data Model**
Add to `users`:

```sql
cross_thread_sharing_default text
  CHECK null OR in ('opt_in', 'opt_out')
```

Default is `NULL`.

Add to bridge candidates:

```sql
bridge_candidates (
  id uuid primary key,
  source_user_id uuid not null,
  target_user_id uuid not null,
  kind text not null,
  status text not null default 'pending',
  sensitivity text not null default 'medium',
  source_message_ids uuid[] default '{}',
  related_memory_ids uuid[] default '{}',
  related_observation_ids uuid[] default '{}',
  internal_note text not null,
  shareable_summary text not null,
  created_at timestamptz default now(),
  updated_at timestamptz default now(),
  resolved_at timestamptz
)
```

Keep enums small:

```text
kind: context | clarification | contradiction | repair | vulnerability | process
status: pending | ready | sent | declined | blocked | addressed | expired
sensitivity: low | medium | high
```

**Visibility Rules**
Every read path should know the current viewer:

```python
viewer_user_id = ctx.user.id
partner_user_id = ctx.partner.id
```

For source-owned material:

```text
source == viewer:
  visible

source == partner and partner sharing == opt_in:
  visible unless OOB/sensitivity blocks

source == partner and partner sharing == opt_out:
  hidden except approved bridge_candidates.shareable_summary

source == partner and partner sharing == unset:
  hidden except process metadata and approved bridge summaries
```

This must apply to:

- hot context
- `search_messages`
- `recent_activity`
- `get_memories`
- `get_observations`
- `get_bot_actions`

That is the important part. The prompt is not the wall. The tools must enforce it.

**Prompt Context**
Hot context should not show every bridge. Show only the newest few.

If unset:

```text
## Cross-thread sharing
- current_user_default: unset
- partner_default: opt_in
- priority: ask current user to choose opt_in or opt_out at the first natural opportunity
```

If both set:

```text
## Cross-thread sharing
- current_user_default: opt_in
- partner_default: opt_out
```

Bridge section:

```text
## Bridge candidates
- showing newest 3 relevant candidates
- use list_bridge_candidates for more
```

Candidate line:

```text
- id=... kind=clarification status=ready sensitivity=medium
  summary: [REDACTED]'s account may clarify why the LSD day still feels loaded for her.
```

**Tools**
Keep tools minimal:

```text
update_cross_thread_sharing_default
create_bridge_candidate
list_bridge_candidates
update_bridge_candidate
send_bridge_candidate
```

No separate approve/decline tools yet. `update_bridge_candidate(status=declined/addressed/blocked)` is enough.

**Agent Behavior**
When current user setting is `unset`, it should ask prominently but not derail urgent moments:

> “Before I keep using both sides this way, I should ask you something clearly. Do you want me to treat what you tell me as shareable by default with [REDACTED], unless you mark something private, or private by default unless you explicitly ask me to share it?”

If partner is opted in, say:

> “[REDACTED] has opted in by default, meaning I can use what she tells me to help you understand her perspective unless she marks something private.”

If partner is opted out:

> “[REDACTED] has opted out by default, meaning I treat what she tells me as private unless she explicitly asks me to share something.”

**Bridge Candidate Creation**
In Phase B, create a bridge candidate when something from one side:

- explains the other side’s conflict
- contradicts or corrects a story
- reveals a vulnerability the other is missing
- could repair a misunderstanding
- creates a process/transparency issue with the assistant

But don’t send automatically. Candidate first, delivery later.

**Implementation Phases**
Phase 1:
Add setting, tool, hot context nudge. Treat all existing users as unset.

Phase 2:
Add privacy helper and apply it to `search_messages`, `recent_activity`, hot context.

Phase 3:
Apply same filtering to memories, observations, bot actions.

Phase 4:
Add bridge candidate table/tools and show newest few in hot context.

Phase 5:
Tests:
- unset partner: raw partner messages never appear in search.
- opt_out partner: raw partner messages never appear.
- opt_in partner: partner messages can appear unless OOB.
- approved bridge: target sees `shareable_summary`, not raw source.
- hot context only shows newest few bridges.
- agent can set opt-in/opt-out via tool.

That keeps it simple while making the privacy boundary real the whole way down.

Yes, but I want to be precise about “proper permissions”: for this pass it means the agent and its tools treat partner-private raw messages as inaccessible unless that partner has explicitly opted in. `unset` and `opt_out` both block raw partner message visibility in prompts and read/search tools; bridge candidates expose only the prepared `shareable_summary` to the target side, not source message text or internal notes.

```json
{
  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: app/services/prompts.py\n@@\n If the user chooses, call `update_cross_thread_sharing_default` in Phase B. Do not infer the setting from vague comfort or discomfort; get an explicit choice. OOB always overrides opt-in.\n \n+# Bridge Candidates\n+\n+Use bridge candidates for cross-thread material that may help the other partner understand, repair, clarify, or contextualize something. This is the permission-aware bridge path; do not manually copy raw partner-private text into the other user's answer.\n+\n+Create a bridge candidate when one partner says something that materially explains, contradicts, clarifies, softens, or adds important context to something the other partner has said, and a shareable version may help. Link the source message ids when possible. Use `shareable_summary` for the neutral, non-inflammatory wording; keep private/raw reasoning in `internal_note`.\n+\n+Lifecycle statuses are exactly `pending`, `ready`, `sent`, `declined`, `blocked`, `addressed`, and `expired`. Use `send_bridge_candidate` to send a `ready` candidate; it sends only the `shareable_summary` through the guarded outbound path. If the source user is unset or opt-out, create `pending` unless they explicitly authorize this specific bridge. High-sensitivity material should stay pending or blocked until it is safe.\n+\n # Tool Usage Philosophy\n@@\n - `recent_activity`: use for a compact cross-thread recent digest; do not use when exact wording matters. Example: see what each partner discussed this week.\n+- `list_bridge_candidates`: use to inspect pending/ready/sent bridge material for this dyad. Target-facing candidates expose shareable summaries only.\n@@\n - `update_cross_thread_sharing_default`: use when the current user explicitly chooses whether their thread is shareable across the relationship bridge by default. `opt_in` means you may use their perspective with the partner when it helps, unless OOB blocks it. `opt_out` means their thread is private by default; only bridge specific material they explicitly ask or allow you to share.\n+- `create_bridge_candidate`: use when a partner's private-thread material may need to be bridged carefully. Write a neutral `shareable_summary`; do not place raw private text there.\n+- `update_bridge_candidate`: use to mark a candidate ready, declined, blocked, addressed, expired, or to improve the summary/note.\n+- `send_bridge_candidate`: use only for `ready` candidates; this is the only tool for sending bridge candidates across threads.\n - `add_memory`: use for a new fact after searching; do not use for patterns. Example: store a concrete family or schedule fact.\n*** End Patch\n"
}
```

> AGENT

Yes. Locally, this is now implemented and tested.

What’s included:
- `unset` / `opt_in` / `opt_out` sharing default on users.
- Tool to update sharing default.
- Permission helper for raw cross-thread message visibility.
- `search_messages`, `recent_activity`, and hot context now hide raw partner content unless that partner is `opt_in`.
- Minimal `bridge_candidates` table and lifecycle.
- `create_bridge_candidate`, `list_bridge_candidates`, `update_bridge_candidate`, `send_bridge_candidate`.
- Bridge sends use only `shareable_summary`, go through OOB/outbound protection, and never send `internal_note`.
- Prompt guidance for unset nudges and when to create/send bridge candidates.
- Tests for invalid `offered` status, permission gating, bridge lifecycle, and send behavior.

Verification:
- Full test suite: `276 passed, 3 skipped`
- Direct privacy reproduction: passed. Partner raw text is hidden while unset, then visible after `opt_in`.

Important caveat: this is not pushed/deployed yet, and the DB migrations still need applying in the target environment:
- `migrations/0012_cross_thread_sharing.sql`
- `migrations/0013_bridge_candidates.sql`

One deliberate limitation remains: old memories/observations are not treated as a hard privacy boundary because the schema still does not prove source ownership. The new bridge candidates do have explicit source/target provenance, so that’s the safe bridge path.

The next gap is more subtle: hot context was safe, but too conservative. It only fetched the current user’s thread, so partner opt-in could not actually surface partner raw recent messages there. I’m changing that path to fetch the dyad’s recent messages and then apply the visibility helper row by row, so unset/opt-out are hidden and opt-in can be used.

I found another permission edge in the write tools: a target-side agent could theoretically update a bridge candidate it knows by id. I’m tightening update permissions so only the source side can edit summaries/internal notes/mark ready; the target side can only mark a visible bridge as addressed or declined.

> AGENT

I got this to a much higher confidence level and fixed several issues found during review.

What I tightened:
- `search_messages` now selects `recipient_id`, so outbound thread ownership is handled correctly.
- Sharing default updates can only update the current user’s setting, not the partner’s.
- Bridge candidates can only be created from the current user as source.
- Target-side bridge updates are restricted: target can only mark a visible candidate `addressed` or `declined`; they cannot rewrite summaries, internal notes, links, sensitivity, or mark it ready.
- Hot context now fetches dyad recent messages and applies the visibility helper row by row:
  - partner `unset` / `opt_out`: raw content hidden
  - partner `opt_in`: raw content can appear
- `recent_activity` is limited to the current dyad only.
- Manual privacy matrix confirms raw partner content does not leak through search, recent activity, hot context, or bridge send.

Verification now:
- Full tests: `277 passed, 3 skipped`
- Focused privacy/bridge/agentic tests: `61 passed`
- Compile check: clean
- `git diff --check`: clean
- Manual privacy matrix: passed

Still not pushed/deployed. The operational next step is commit, push, deploy, then apply:
- `migrations/0012_cross_thread_sharing.sql`
- `migrations/0013_bridge_candidates.sql`

> AGENT

I’ll look this up against current OpenAI docs, then map it onto the existing media/message pipeline before editing. After that I’ll implement the smallest durable path: an explain-style media analysis tool/call, persisted on the message row, and searchable later.

> AGENT

The OpenAI docs point to the Responses API for image understanding: send image URLs or base64/data URLs as `input_image`, with `detail` set to `high` or `original` where supported. For this repo, the right fit is to analyze stored media when a message arrives, save that analysis into the existing message media fields, and expose it through the existing message search/hot-context paths.

return result\n+    owner_id = _message_thread_owner_id(row)\n+    sharing_default = ctx.user.cross_thread_sharing_default if owner_id == ctx.user.id else ctx.partner.cross_thread_sharing_default\n+    if not raw_message_visibility(\n+        viewer_user_id=ctx.user.id,\n+        thread_owner_user_id=owner_id,\n+        thread_owner_sharing_default=sharing_default,\n+    ).visible:\n+        result = ExplainMediaItemOutput(\n+            action=\"blocked\",\n+            message_id=args.message_id,\n+            media_type=row[\"media_type\"],\n+            reason=\"raw partner media hidden by sharing_default\",\n+        )\n+        await _log_tool_call(ctx, \"explain_media_item\", args, started, result)\n+        return result\n+    if row[\"media_type\"] != \"image\" or not row[\"media_url\"]:\n+        result = ExplainMediaItemOutput(\n+            action=\"unsupported\",\n+            message_id=args.message_id,\n+            media_type=row[\"media_type\"],\n+            reason=\"only stored image media can be explained right now\",\n+        )\n+        await _log_tool_call(ctx, \"explain_media_item\", args, started, result)\n+        return result\n+\n+    try:\n+        analysis = await explain_stored_image(ctx.pool, args.message_id)\n+    except Exception as exc:\n+        result = ExplainMediaItemOutput(\n+            action=\"unsupported\",\n+            message_id=args.message_id,\n+            media_type=row[\"media_type\"],\n+            reason=f\"media explanation failed: {exc}\",\n+        )\n+        await _log_tool_call(ctx, \"explain_media_item\", args, started, result)\n+        return result\n+\n+    result = ExplainMediaItemOutput(\n+        action=\"explained\",\n+        message_id=args.message_id,\n+        media_type=row[\"media_type\"],\n+        explanation=analysis.get(\"explanation\") or analysis.get(\"description\"),\n+        reason=args.reason,\n+    )\n+    await _log_tool_call(ctx, \"explain_media_item\", args, started, result)\n+    return result\n+\n+\n async def log_feedback(ctx: TurnContext, args: LogFeedbackInput) -> LogFeedbackOutput:\n*** End Patch\n"

```json
  "patch": "*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: /tmp/Veas-work/app/services/prompts.py\n@@\n - `delete_outbound_message`: use only when one of your already-sent messages should not remain visible, such as accidental protected detail, wrong recipient, serious factual mistake, or a message that would predictably worsen the situation. Prefer editing when the message can be safely corrected.\n - `react_to_message`: use when an emoji reaction is the most natural response or useful alongside a short reply. Call `search_emojis` first when the right reaction is not obvious, then choose a precise, emotionally apt, sometimes unusual emoji that fits the exact meaning better than generic 👍/❤️/👋. Do not overuse reactions, and do not choose cute or obscure emoji when the moment is serious.\n+- `explain_media_item`: use when a stored image needs a fresh durable explanation. It calls image understanding and saves the explanation into message memory so `search_messages` can find it later.\n - `log_feedback`: use when the user gives feedback about your output or behavior; do not convert every emotional reaction into feedback.\n*** End Patch\n"
}
```

What is verified:
- Implemented durable image explanations using OpenAI Responses API image input with `detail=high`, default model `gpt-5.5`.
- Saved explanation into `messages.media_analysis.explanation` plus legacy `description`.
- Made `search_messages` query those saved explanations.
- Made hot context render media explanations when message text is empty.
- Added `explain_media_item` tool to re-run image understanding on stored images, with sharing-default privacy gating.
- Full test suite passed: `280 passed, 3 skipped`.
- Pushed commit: `ea3eba8`.
- Railway deployment succeeded: `99b88ed1-312c-4edc-a97b-e7d9b8f4ea0f`.

I also checked deployment logs: startup completed and Railway `/health` returned `200 OK`.

The only thing not proven by tests is an actual live OpenAI image-understanding call in production with your real API key and a real uploaded media object. The implementation matches OpenAI’s current docs: Responses API supports image inputs, `input_image` can use a data URL, and `detail` can be `high`; OpenAI’s model docs say latest models support image input and vision.

Sources:
- OpenAI image/vision guide: https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/images-vision
- OpenAI Responses API reference: https://platform.openai.com/docs/api-reference/responses
- OpenAI models docs: https://platform.openai.com/docs/models

> DEVELOPER

which model do you use?

> AGENT

For the new media explanation path I set it to use `gpt-5.5` by default.

It’s configurable via the new env var:

`VISION_MODEL=gpt-5.5`

and image detail is:

`VISION_DETAIL=high`

So production will use `gpt-5.5` for image understanding unless you override `VISION_MODEL` in Railway.

> DEVELOPER

> AGENT

> AGENT

Not as a general agent tool right now.

What it does have:
- Main conversational loop: Anthropic `CONVERSATIONAL_MODEL`, currently Claude.
- Separate LLM checks for specific jobs:
  - OOB checker for outbound safety.
  - Observation significance scorer.
  - Pacing/judgement helper.
  - OpenAI vision for image explanations.
  - Groq Whisper for transcription.
- Eval/judge code exists for testing, but that is not exposed to the live agent as “ask another mind.”

What it does **not** currently have:
- No `second_opinion` tool.
- No “ask a different persona” tool.
- No multi-model panel / critic / mediator board.
- No persistent “consultation result” object that the agent can later cite or search.

I’d add it as a read-phase tool, probably called something like `consult_panel` or `get_second_opinion`.

Shape I’d recommend:
- Inputs: `question`, `context_summary`, `mode`
  - `mode`: `careful_mediator`, `skeptical_reviewer`, `avoidance_barrier_checker`, `repair_wording_coach`, maybe `panel`.
- Strict privacy gate: it should only receive hot context and raw messages the current user is allowed to see.
- Output: structured opinions, not final truth:
  - `take`
  - `risks`
  - `what_to_ask_next`
  - `wording_suggestions`
  - `confidence`
- Optional persistence: save the consult result into `tool_calls` automatically, and optionally into a new `consultations` table if we want it searchable/auditable later.

I would not let it freely query partner-private material. It should go through the same sharing/OOB constraints as `search_messages` and bridge candidates, otherwise it becomes a side door around the permissions work we just added.

> AGENT

Yes. The right abstraction is probably **perspective agents**: same underlying memory/search/context tools, different interpretive frame, and constrained authority.

High-level shape:

The main agent stays the **orchestrator**. It owns the conversation, user trust, privacy rules, bridge decisions, and any writes/sends. A subagent is spun up for a bounded question like:

> “Look at this situation through an IFS-informed lens. What might each person’s protective parts be trying to do, what would you ask next, and what should we avoid saying?”

The subagent can use the same read tools, but I would be careful about write tools. My strong recommendation:

- Subagents can use **read tools**: messages, memories, observations, themes, OOB summaries, bridge candidates, self model.
- Subagents cannot directly send, edit, delete, react, update memory, create bridge candidates, or escalate.
- Subagents return a structured consult result.
- The main agent decides what to do with it.

Otherwise you get multiple agents all mutating state or sending messages, which is risky in a relationship context.

**Perspective Templates**
I’d define a small registry of perspective templates. Examples:

- `ifs_parts_lens`
  Looks for protective parts, wounded parts, polarization, shame/defensiveness loops, what each part is trying to protect.

- `sex_therapist_lens`
  Looks at desire, rejection sensitivity, initiation patterns, safety, pressure, consent, shame, avoidance, erotic context. Should be careful and non-pathologizing.

- `habits_and_daily_life_lens`
  Looks at routines, energy, friction points, timing, household patterns, habit loops, implementation details.

- `attachment_lens`
  Looks at pursue/withdraw, protest behavior, reassurance bids, distance regulation, threat responses.

- `conflict_mediator_lens`
  Looks at interests vs positions, hearable translations, repair moves, sequencing.

- `avoidance_barrier_checker`
  Specifically asks: “What wording would trigger defensiveness, insult, shame, pressure, or avoidance?”

- `bridge_translation_lens`
  Asks: “If this should be carried to the partner, what is the least inflammatory true version?”

- `skeptical_evidence_reviewer`
  Pushes back on overinterpretation. “What are we assuming? What evidence is thin? What alternative explanations exist?”

- `trauma_informed_lens`
  Looks for threat, overwhelm, shutdown, control, safety, pacing. Should explicitly avoid diagnosing.

**How A Consult Should Return**
I’d make every perspective return the same shape:

```json
{
  "perspective": "ifs_parts_lens",
  "take": "Short core read.",
  "useful_reframes": ["..."],
  "risks": ["..."],
  "questions_to_ask": ["..."],
  "possible_bridge": {
    "should_bridge": true,
    "summary": "...",
    "sensitivity": "medium"
  },
  "what_not_to_say": ["..."],
  "confidence": "medium",
  "evidence_used": ["message ids / memory ids / observation ids"]
}
```

That structure matters because the main agent can compare outputs without swallowing one persona’s framing as truth.

**Same Tools, Same Permissions**
The important bit: “same tools” cannot mean “same raw access regardless of context.” It should mean:

- Same read interface.
- Same OOB constraints.
- Same cross-thread sharing constraints.
- Same search visibility rules.
- Same bridge candidate visibility rules.

So if the current user is not allowed to see partner-private raw material, neither is the subagent. The subagent gets the same filtered world the main agent gets.

**When To Use It**
The main agent should not consult constantly. It should use this when:

- The situation is charged or ambiguous.
- The user asks for another lens.
- There is a bridge candidate or cross-thread contradiction.
- The agent is considering a sensitive reply.
- The user is stuck in a repeated pattern.
- Sexual intimacy, shame, family, trauma, or high-avoidance dynamics are involved.

It should not use it for simple support, scheduling, or obvious replies.

**Single Lens vs Panel**
I’d support both.

Single lens:

> “Ask the IFS lens about this.”

Panel:

> “Ask IFS, attachment, sex therapist, and skeptical reviewer; then synthesize.”

But the panel should be capped. Maybe 2-4 perspectives max, otherwise it becomes slow, expensive, and muddy.

**Best Architecture**
I’d probably add:

- `app/services/perspectives.py`
  Registry of templates.

- `consult_perspective` read tool
  Runs one perspective subagent with read tools.

- `consult_panel` read tool
  Runs multiple perspectives and returns a synthesis.

- Optional later table: `consultations`
  Stores durable consults if we want audit/search.

The main agent would see prompt guidance like:

> Use perspective consults when a different lens would materially improve judgment. Treat consults as advisory, not authoritative. Do not outsource responsibility for privacy, safety, or final wording.

That gets you what you want: same memory/tool substrate, different interpretive minds, but without letting a subagent accidentally become a second uncontrolled relationship agent.

> AGENT

I’ll use the `megaplan` skill lightly here, but keep this execution-focused rather than turning it into a big planning ceremony.

> AGENT

Yes. Light implementation plan:

**Goal**
Add a read-phase tool that lets the main agent ask a custom-perspective subagent for advice, using the same safe read tools and filtered context, then returns a structured consult. Main agent keeps final responsibility.

**Tool Shape**
Add one primary tool:

`consult_perspective`

Inputs:
- `question`: what the current agent wants help thinking through.
- `perspective`: free-form custom lens, e.g. “IFS-informed therapist”, “sex therapist”, “habits consultant”, “skeptical evidence reviewer”.
- `focus`: optional, e.g. `bridge_wording`, `pattern_understanding`, `what_to_ask_next`, `risk_check`.
- `message_ids`: optional source messages to center.
- `include_panel`: probably false initially.

Output:
- `perspective`
- `core_read`
- `alternative_interpretations`
- `risks_or_blindspots`
- `questions_to_ask`
- `bridge_relevance`
- `what_not_to_say`
- `confidence`
- `evidence_used`

**Architecture**
1. Add schemas in `tool_schemas.py`.
2. Add `consult_perspective` to `READ_PHASE_TOOLS`.
3. Implement `app/services/perspective_consults.py`.
4. The consult runner spins up a bounded Anthropic call with:
   - perspective-specific system prompt
   - current hot context
   - tool access limited to read tools only
   - no outbound/write tools
5. Persist the consult automatically through normal `tool_calls`; optionally add a `consultations` table later if we want searchable consult history.

**Permissions**
Important rule: subagents do not get privileged access.

They see:
- same hot context as main agent
- same `search_messages` filtering
- same OOB rules
- same sharing-default visibility
- same bridge candidate visibility

They cannot:
- send messages
- edit/delete/react
- create bridge candidates
- write memories/observations
- escalate

**Prompt Templates**
Start with a small registry plus custom free-form perspective:

- `custom`
- `ifs_parts_lens`
- `sex_therapist_lens`
- `habits_daily_life_lens`
- `attachment_lens`
- `conflict_mediator_lens`
- `avoidance_barrier_checker`
- `bridge_translation_lens`
- `skeptical_evidence_reviewer`

The user can ask directly: “can you ask a sex therapist lens?” The agent can also use it when appropriate.

**Main Prompt Update**
Tell the main agent:

Use `consult_perspective` when:
- the user asks for another lens
- the situation is charged/ambiguous
- bridge wording might land badly
- sexual intimacy, shame, family, trauma, avoidance, or recurring patterns are involved
- the agent may be overfitting one interpretation

Treat consults as advisory, not truth.

**Testing**
Add tests for:
- tool registry exposes it in read phase only
- write phase rejects it
- custom perspective prompt is passed through
- subagent can call read tools but not write tools
- partner-private raw messages stay hidden under opt-out/unset
- output validates into structured schema
- consult failures return a safe error, not a crashed turn

**First Implementation Cut**
Keep it simple:
- one tool: `consult_perspective`
- one model setting: `perspective_consult_model`, default same as conversational model
- read tools only
- no new database table yet
- persisted only through `tool_calls`
- templates in code as a dict

Then later:
- `consult_panel`
- durable `consultations` table
- reusable saved perspectives
- automatic “panel on bridge candidates” behavior.

Implemented, pushed, and deployed.

What changed:
- Added read-phase `consult_perspective` tool.
- Supports named templates plus custom perspective text.
- Consult subagent can use safe read tools only.
- It cannot send, write, escalate, react, edit/delete, or recursively consult.
- It inherits hot context, privacy filtering, protected owner IDs, and consult-phase OOB defaults.
- Added bounded consult settings:
  - `CONSULT_MODEL`, defaults to `CONVERSATIONAL_MODEL`
  - `CONSULT_MAX_TOOL_ITERATIONS`
  - `CONSULT_TIMEOUT_S`
- Added structured advisory output with graceful error handling.
- Updated system prompt/tool guidance.
- Added focused tests.
